People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Dr. [Name], I'm here because... well, honestly, it's because people keep acting like I'm making things up, like I'm just imagining the whole thing, but I know what I see. I've been a housekeeper for a long time, you know, scrubbing floors and dusting baseboards for the agency, and I know when things are out of place or when someone's been around. And lately, it's just been so obvious to me. The hospital people, and even some folks from my own cleaning agency, they've been... they've been watching our house. And not just the house, but my cleaning routes too. I see the same cars parked down the street when I leave for work, and then again when I get to my clients' houses. It's like they're tracking my every move. And I know why they're doing it, too. They're trying to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband. He's been having some health issues, and I do everything for him, I really do, but it's like they don't believe me. They think they need to spy on me to make sure I'm doing my job as a wife. It's exhausting, really, always looking over my shoulder, wondering who's watching me while I'm just trying to earn a living and take care of my home. But no one wants to believe me when I tell them. They just give me that look, you know the one, like I'm crazy or something. But I'm not. I know what's happening.

Patient reports being monitored/tracked by multiple parties (hospital staff, cleaning agency) and attributes this to concerns about her caregiving. Maintains conviction despite others' disbelief. Key features: non-bizarre delusions, organized thought, functional (still working), no mention of hallucinations yet, possible delusional system centered on perceived surveillance. F22.0 (Delusional disorder, erotomanic type - less likely but possible given caregiver monitoring theme), F22.9 (other/unspecified delusional disorder - most likely given persecutory content), F24 (Folie à deux - if husband shares beliefs), F20.0 (Paranoid schizophrenia - if other psychotic features present), F20.2 (Catatonic schizophrenia - less likely given presentation). ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mistaken? No, no, Dr. [Name], I'm not mistaken. I know what you're getting at, it's what everyone gets at, that little hint of doubt they try to plant in your head. But you have to understand, I've been doing this for thirty years. Thirty years of driving the same routes, parking in the same driveways, noticing the little things. When you clean houses for a living, you develop an eye for detail. You notice if a vase has been moved half an inch, or if there's a scuff mark on the baseboard that wasn't there last Tuesday. So when I tell you I'm seeing the same cars, I mean I am seeing the exact same cars. There's this one, a dark blue sedan, always parked near the corner when I leave my house at 6:45 AM. And then, wouldn't you know it, when I pull up to Mrs. Higgins' house across town at 8:00, there it is again, just idling two doors down. And it's not just the cars, it's the people in them. I can feel them looking at me. 

Like I said before, I know exactly why they're doing it. It's the hospital people and the agency folks. They've teamed up or something because of my husband. He had that bad spell with his heart last year, and ever since then, I just know they've been whispering about me. They think because I work long hours, I'm neglecting him. But I'm not! I make his meals, I organize his pills, I do everything. But they don't believe it. So they watch the house, they watch my routes, they're just waiting for me to slip up so they can say, 'Aha! See? She's not taking proper care of him!' It's all a big test, a big surveillance operation just to catch me doing something wrong. So no, doctor, I'm not mistaken. I wish I was, I really do, because then maybe I could just go to work and come home without feeling like I'm on trial every single day. But I'm not imagining it. I know what I see.

He had that bad spell with his heart last year, and ever since then, I just know they've been whispering about me. They think because I work long hours, I'm neglecting him. But I'm not! I make his meals, I organize his pills, I do everything. But they don't believe it. So they watch the house, they watch my routes, they're just waiting for me to slip up so they can say, 'Aha! See? She's not taking proper care of him!' It's all a big test, a big surveillance operation just to catch me doing something wrong. So no, doctor, I'm not mistaken. I wish I was, I really do, because then maybe I could just go to work and come home without feeling like I'm on trial every single day. But I'm not imagining it. I know what I see.\n\n[Last Question]\nDoes your husband also believe that you're being watched by the hospital people and the agency, or does he think these concerns are just in your head?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / maid_or_housekeeping_cleaner / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I'm here because people keep acting like I'm imagining it, but I know the hospital people and some folks from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my husband... see, that's the thing that really breaks my heart, Dr. [Name]. Because you'd think, after thirty-two years of marriage, you'd think the person you share a bed with, the person you cook for and clean for and worry over every single day, you'd think he would be on your side. But he's just like the rest of them. He tells me I'm working too hard, that the chemicals I use at work are getting to my head, or that I'm just stressed about his health. Can you believe that? Stressed! Of course I'm stressed! But not because I'm imagining things. 

Like I told you, I know exactly what's happening. People keep acting like I'm making it all up, like I'm just imagining the whole thing, but I know the hospital people and those folks from the agency have been watching our house. They're watching my cleaning routes. They are actively doing this to see if I'm taking proper care of him. And I try to explain it to him, I sit him down at the kitchen table—the one I polished just that morning, mind you—and I say, "Frank, look out the window, look at that blue car, they are waiting for me to leave for Mrs. Higgins' house." And he just sighs. He does this heavy sigh, rubs his face, and tells me to take a pill and lie down. He thinks it's all in my head. It makes me feel so alone, doctor. Because if your own husband doesn't believe you when you're trying to protect him, when you're trying to prove to the whole world that you are a good wife who takes proper care of him despite the agency and the hospital snooping around... well, who is going to believe you? It's just so frustrating, having to defend myself not just to the people spying on me, but to the very man I'm trying to care for.

Because if your own husband doesn't believe you when you're trying to protect him, when you're trying to prove to the whole world that you are a good wife who takes proper care of him despite the agency and the hospital snooping around... well, who is going to believe you? It's just so frustrating, having to defend myself not just to the people spying on me, but to the very man I'm trying to care for.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned the hospital people and the agency folks are watching you. Can you tell me - do you think they're working together as part of an organized plan, or are they acting separately? And what exactly do you think their ultimate goal is - what are they trying to accomplish by watching you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, they are absolutely working together, Dr. [Name]. It’s a coordinated effort, I’m telling you. Think about it—how else would the hospital people know exactly what time I leave for my morning shift? The agency has to be giving them my schedule! It’s the only way it makes sense. I know I keep saying this, and I know people keep acting like I'm just imagining it, like I'm just making up stories out of thin air because I'm tired or stressed. But I know the hospital people and some folks from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes. I've seen them with my own two eyes, day in and day out. 

And as for what they want? Their ultimate goal? It's exactly what I was saying—they want to catch me slipping up. They want to prove that I'm not taking proper care of my husband. They think because I’m out scrubbing floors and cleaning toilets all day, doing back-breaking work for other people, that I’m somehow neglecting my own home and my own husband. But I’m not! I prep his meals before I even leave the house—low sodium, just like his cardiologist ordered. I sort his pills into that little plastic container with the days of the week on it, making sure the morning ones and the evening ones are perfectly separated. I do all the laundry, I keep the house spotless... I mean, I'm a professional cleaner, for heaven's sake, my house is immaculate! 

But they don't care about any of that. They just want to find one little mistake. They want to catch me being five minutes late getting home from a job, or maybe they want to see if I forget to pick up his prescription, just so they can swoop in and say, "Look, she’s an unfit wife, she can't handle it." Maybe the hospital wants to force him into one of those expensive care facilities, or maybe the agency just wants a reason to let me go because they think my home life is a liability now, I don't know the exact paperwork of it all. But I know they are working together to build some kind of case against me. It’s a full-time job just trying to outsmart them and keep my head on a swivel while I’m doing my actual job, you know? It’s just... it's not right. I work too hard for my family to be treated like some kind of criminal.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, that's exactly what I try to lay out for people, Dr. [Name], because the way they communicate is all hidden in the paperwork and the schedules. I try to explain it, I really do, because it’s all connected. But it’s so frustrating, because when I try to explain what is actually happening, how they're passing the information back and forth, the words just... they run together because there are so many moving pieces. 

I start trying to explain about my husband's heart pills—the blue ones, you know, not the white ones, because the pharmacy changed the manufacturer on the 14th, and that refill date lines up exactly with when the hospital people must have called the agency. But then, to understand that, you have to understand the church schedule. Because Mrs. Higgins is on the altar guild, and the hospital auxiliary board meets on the exact same Tuesdays that the church prints the new bulletins, which is how the hospital knows when I'm going to be on the east side of town. And then... well, then it runs right into the numbers on my client list. The agency gives me these routing numbers for the houses I clean, and if you look at the last two digits of my client list, they match the hospital extension for the cardiology department! 

It's all right there! But when I try to explain this to Frank, or to anyone really, how the heart pills connect to the church schedule and then to the numbers on my client list... people just sigh and say they can't follow me. They tell me I'm jumping around too much. But I'm not jumping around, I'm just trying to give them the whole picture! You have to see all the pieces to understand how the hospital knows where the agency is sending me. If I don't explain the church schedule, then the client numbers don't make sense, and if I leave out the heart pills, then the whole timeline falls apart. But nobody has the patience to just sit and listen to the details. They just wave their hands and say they can't follow what I'm saying, when really, they just aren't paying close enough attention to the connections I'm trying to show them.

She's not taking proper care of him!' It's all a big test, a big surveillance operation just to catch me doing something wrong. So no, doctor, I'm not mistaken. I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Does your husband also believe that you're being watched by the hospital people and the agency, or does he think these concerns are just in your head?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband... see, that's the thing that really breaks my heart, Dr. [Name]. Because you'd think, after thirty-two years of marriage, you'd think the person you share a bed with, the person you cook for and clean for and worry over every single day, you'd think he would be on your side. But he's just like the rest of them. He tells me I'm working too hard, that the chemicals I use at work are getting to my head, or that I'm just stressed about his health. Can you believe that? Stressed! Of course I'm stressed! But not because I'm imagining things. \n\nLike I told you, I know exactly what's happening. People keep acting like I'm making it all up, like I'm just imagining the whole thing, but I know the hospital people and those folks from the agency have been watching our house. They're watching my cleaning routes. They are actively doing this to see if I'm taking proper care of him. And I try to explain it to him, I sit him down at the kitchen table—the one I polished just that morning, mind you—and I say, \"Frank, look out the window, look at that blue car, they are waiting for me to leave for Mrs. Higgins' house.\" And he just sighs. He does this heavy sigh, rubs his face, and tells me to take a pill and lie down. He thinks it's all in my head. It makes me feel so alone, doctor. Because if your own husband doesn't believe you when you're trying to protect him, when you're trying to prove to the whole world that you are a good wife who takes proper care of him despite the agency and the hospital snooping around... well, who is going to believe you? It's just so frustrating, having to defend myself not just to the people spying on me, but to the very man I'm trying to care for.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the hospital people and the agency folks are watching you. Can you tell me - do you think they're working together as part of an organized plan, or are they acting separately? And what exactly do you think their ultimate goal is - what are they trying to accomplish by watching you?\n\nPatient: Oh, they are absolutely working together, Dr. [Name]. It’s a coordinated effort, I’m telling you. Think about it—how else would the hospital people know exactly what time I leave for my morning shift? The agency has to be giving them my schedule! It’s the only way it makes sense. I know I keep saying this, and I know people keep acting like I'm just imagining it, like I'm just making up stories out of thin air because I'm tired or stressed. But I know the hospital people and some folks from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes. I've seen them with my own two eyes, day in and day out. \n\nAnd as for what they want? Their ultimate goal? It's exactly what I was saying—they want to catch me slipping up. They want to prove that I'm not taking proper care of my husband. They think because I’m out scrubbing floors and cleaning toilets all day, doing back-breaking work for other people, that I’m somehow neglecting my own home and my own husband. But I’m not! I prep his meals before I even leave the house—low sodium, just like his cardiologist ordered. I sort his pills into that little plastic container with the days of the week on it, making sure the morning ones and the evening ones are perfectly separated. I do all the laundry, I keep the house spotless... I mean, I'm a professional cleaner, for heaven's sake, my house is immaculate! \n\nBut they don't care about any of that. They just want to find one little mistake. They want to catch me being five minutes late getting home from a job, or maybe they want to see if I forget to pick up his prescription, just so they can swoop in and say, \"Look, she’s an unfit wife, she can't handle it.\" Maybe the hospital wants to force him into one of those expensive care facilities, or maybe the agency just wants a reason to let me go because they think my home life is a liability now, I don't know the exact paperwork of it all. But I know they are working together to build some kind of case against me. It’s a full-time job just trying to outsmart them and keep my head on a swivel while I’m doing my actual job, you know? It’s just... it's not right. I work too hard for my family to be treated like some kind of criminal.\n\nDoctor: You've described this coordinated surveillance operation in quite a bit of detail. If I wanted to understand the full picture, could you walk me through exactly how you think the hospital and the agency are communicating with each other? How do you believe they're sharing your schedule and coordinating their surveillance?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's exactly what I try to lay out for people, Dr. [Name], because the way they communicate is all hidden in the paperwork and the schedules. I try to explain it, I really do, because it’s all connected. But it’s so frustrating, because when I try to explain what is actually happening, how they're passing the information back and forth, the words just... they run together because there are so many moving pieces. \n\nI start trying to explain about my husband's heart pills—the blue ones, you know, not the white ones, because the pharmacy changed the manufacturer on the 14th, and that refill date lines up exactly with when the hospital people must have called the agency. But then, to understand that, you have to understand the church schedule. Because Mrs. Higgins is on the altar guild, and the hospital auxiliary board meets on the exact same Tuesdays that the church prints the new bulletins, which is how the hospital knows when I'm going to be on the east side of town. And then... well, then it runs right into the numbers on my client list. The agency gives me these routing numbers for the houses I clean, and if you look at the last two digits of my client list, they match the hospital extension for the cardiology department! \n\nIt's all right there! But when I try to explain this to Frank, or to anyone really, how the heart pills connect to the church schedule and then to the numbers on my client list... people just sigh and say they can't follow me. They tell me I'm jumping around too much. But I'm not jumping around, I'm just trying to give them the whole picture! You have to see all the pieces to understand how the hospital knows where the agency is sending me. If I don't explain the church schedule, then the client numbers don't make sense, and if I leave out the heart pills, then the whole timeline falls apart. But nobody has the patience to just sit and listen to the details. They just wave their hands and say they can't follow what I'm saying, when really, they just aren't paying close enough attention to the connections I'm trying to show them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Oh, it is absolutely one unified plan, doctor. From the very beginning, they had this all mapped out. It’s not just me picking up random puzzle pieces and trying to jam them together, no. They laid the whole puzzle out before I even knew we were playing a game.

If they would just sit down and look at the timeline, they would see that the hospital and the agency sat down together, looked at my life, and built this entire surveillance operation from the ground up just to prove I'm a bad wife. I'm not making connections that aren't there, I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the plan they put in motion! It's all one big, giant net they cast over my life, and I'm just trying my hardest not to get tangled up in it while I do my job and take care of Frank.

But he's just like the rest of them. He tells me I'm working too hard, that the chemicals I use at work are getting to my head, or that I'm just stressed about his health. Can you believe that? Stressed! Of course I'm stressed! But not because I'm imagining things. \n\nLike I told you, I know exactly what's happening. People keep acting like I'm making it all up, like I'm just imagining the whole thing, but I know the hospital people and those folks from the agency have been watching our house. They're watching my cleaning routes. They are actively doing this to see if I'm taking proper care of him. And I try to explain it to him, I sit him down at the kitchen table—the one I polished just that morning, mind you—and I say, \"Frank, look out the window, look at that blue car, they are waiting for me to leave for Mrs. Higgins' house.\" And he just sighs. He does this heavy sigh, rubs his face, and tells me to take a pill and lie down. He thinks it's all in my head. It makes me feel so alone, doctor. Because if your own husband doesn't believe you when you're trying to protect him, when you're trying to prove to the whole world that you are a good wife who takes proper care of him despite the agency and the hospital snooping around... well, who is going to believe you? It's just so frustrating, having to defend myself not just to the people spying on me, but to the very man I'm trying to care for.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the hospital people and the agency folks are watching you. Can you tell me - do you think they're working together as part of an organized plan, or are they acting separately? And what exactly do you think their ultimate goal is - what are they trying to accomplish by watching you?\n\nPatient: Oh, they are absolutely working together, Dr. [Name]. It’s a coordinated effort, I’m telling you. Think about it—how else would the hospital people know exactly what time I leave for my morning shift? The agency has to be giving them my schedule! It’s the only way it makes sense. I know I keep saying this, and I know people keep acting like I'm just imagining it, like I'm just making up stories out of thin air because I'm tired or stressed. But I know the hospital people and some folks from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes. I've seen them with my own two eyes, day in and day out. \n\nAnd as for what they want? Their ultimate goal? It's exactly what I was saying—they want to catch me slipping up. They want to prove that I'm not taking proper care of my husband. They think because I’m out scrubbing floors and cleaning toilets all day, doing back-breaking work for other people, that I’m somehow neglecting my own home and my own husband. But I’m not! I prep his meals before I even leave the house—low sodium, just like his cardiologist ordered. I sort his pills into that little plastic container with the days of the week on it, making sure the morning ones and the evening ones are perfectly separated. I do all the laundry, I keep the house spotless... I mean, I'm a professional cleaner, for heaven's sake, my house is immaculate! \n\nBut they don't care about any of that. They just want to find one little mistake. They want to catch me being five minutes late getting home from a job, or maybe they want to see if I forget to pick up his prescription, just so they can swoop in and say, \"Look, she’s an unfit wife, she can't handle it.\" Maybe the hospital wants to force him into one of those expensive care facilities, or maybe the agency just wants a reason to let me go because they think my home life is a liability now, I don't know the exact paperwork of it all. But I know they are working together to build some kind of case against me. It’s a full-time job just trying to outsmart them and keep my head on a swivel while I’m doing my actual job, you know? It’s just... it's not right. I work too hard for my family to be treated like some kind of criminal.\n\nDoctor: You've described this coordinated surveillance operation in quite a bit of detail. If I wanted to understand the full picture, could you walk me through exactly how you think the hospital and the agency are communicating with each other? How do you believe they're sharing your schedule and coordinating their surveillance?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's exactly what I try to lay out for people, Dr. [Name], because the way they communicate is all hidden in the paperwork and the schedules. I try to explain it, I really do, because it’s all connected. But it’s so frustrating, because when I try to explain what is actually happening, how they're passing the information back and forth, the words just... they run together because there are so many moving pieces. \n\nI start trying to explain about my husband's heart pills—the blue ones, you know, not the white ones, because the pharmacy changed the manufacturer on the 14th, and that refill date lines up exactly with when the hospital people must have called the agency. But then, to understand that, you have to understand the church schedule. Because Mrs. Higgins is on the altar guild, and the hospital auxiliary board meets on the exact same Tuesdays that the church prints the new bulletins, which is how the hospital knows when I'm going to be on the east side of town. And then... well, then it runs right into the numbers on my client list. The agency gives me these routing numbers for the houses I clean, and if you look at the last two digits of my client list, they match the hospital extension for the cardiology department! \n\nIt's all right there! But when I try to explain this to Frank, or to anyone really, how the heart pills connect to the church schedule and then to the numbers on my client list... people just sigh and say they can't follow me. They tell me I'm jumping around too much. But I'm not jumping around, I'm just trying to give them the whole picture! You have to see all the pieces to understand how the hospital knows where the agency is sending me. If I don't explain the church schedule, then the client numbers don't make sense, and if I leave out the heart pills, then the whole timeline falls apart. But nobody has the patience to just sit and listen to the details. They just wave their hands and say they can't follow what I'm saying, when really, they just aren't paying close enough attention to the connections I'm trying to show them.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at everything you've told me—the blue car, the hospital, the agency, the church schedule, the pill numbers—do these things feel like separate incidents that you're connecting together, or do they feel like one unified plan that was already organized from the start?\n\nPatient: Oh, it is absolutely one unified plan, doctor. From the very beginning, they had this all mapped out. It’s not just me picking up random puzzle pieces and trying to jam them together, no. They laid the whole puzzle out before I even knew we were playing a game. \n\nI mean, I know exactly why I'm sitting here talking to you. I'm here because people keep acting like I'm imagining it all, like I'm just a tired old woman who's losing her grip on reality from breathing in too much pine cleaner and floor wax all these years. But I know what I know. I know the hospital people and some of those folks from the agency have been watching our house and tracking my cleaning routes. And they didn't just decide to do that on a whim or as separate little things. It was organized from the start, all entirely designed to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband. \n\nThink about it, the blue car didn't just magically appear one day by accident. It showed up right when the pill numbers changed, which was right when the church schedule shifted. They set the whole board up to trap me from day one. And it just... it really drives me up the wall that my own husband, and everyone else for that matter, treats me like I'm making up fairy tales. If they would just sit down and look at the timeline, they would see that the hospital and the agency sat down together, looked at my life, and built this entire surveillance operation from the ground up just to prove I'm a bad wife. I'm not making connections that aren't there, I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the plan they put in motion! It's all one big, giant net they cast over my life, and I'm just trying my hardest not to get tangled up in it while I do my job and take care of Frank.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It's all one big, giant net they cast over my life, and I'm just trying my hardest not to get tangled up in it while I do my job and take care of Frank.\n\n[Last Question]\nBesides seeing the cars and connecting these things together, do you ever hear voices or sounds that other people don't seem to hear, or do you experience anything else unusual like that?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / maid_or_housekeeping_cleaner / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"Even when the house is quiet, I hear a woman's voice near the kitchen window telling me to check the locks or move his pills, clear as if she is standing right there.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... you know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. I haven't really told Frank this part because he already looks at me like I've got two heads, but... yes, I do hear something. 

It happens mostly when I'm in the kitchen. You know how a house gets when it's really, truly quiet? Like, early in the morning before the sun is even up and I'm getting Frank's low-sodium breakfast ready, or late at night after I've finished my last cleaning shift and I'm just wiping down our own counters because I can't settle my mind. The house will be completely, entirely silent. You could hear a pin drop. But then... right by the kitchen window—the one over the sink, where I keep my African violets—I hear this voice. 

It's a woman's voice. And she's not whispering or mumbling, Dr. [Name], she is speaking clear as day, just as clear as you and I are talking right now. It sounds exactly like she is standing right there on the linoleum next to me. And she tells me things I need to do. She'll say, plain as anything, that I need to check the locks, or she'll tell me to move Frank's pills. She'll say, "Check the back door," or "Move his pills off that counter." 

And I'll stop dead in my tracks, usually with a dish towel still in my hand, and I'll look around, expecting to see someone right there in my kitchen. But there's nobody there. Just me and the quiet house. But I know what I heard! It's so real that I actually go and check the deadbolt on the back door, and I'll take his little plastic pill container and move it to the other side of the kitchen, just like she said. Because, well, with everything else going on, with the hospital and the agency watching me, you just can't be too careful, right? But it really startles me, because I'll just be standing there in the dead quiet, and suddenly this woman is right there at the window telling me to secure the house and hide the medication. I just... I don't know how she gets so close without me seeing her, but I hear her, clear as a bell.

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. [Name], I'm here because... well, honestly, it's because people keep acting like I'm making things up, like I'm just imagining the whole thing, but I know what I see. I've been a housekeeper for a long time, you know, scrubbing floors and dusting baseboards for the agency, and I know when things are out of place or when someone's been around. And lately, it's just been so obvious to me. The hospital people, and even some folks from my own cleaning agency, they've been... they've been watching our house. And not just the house, but my cleaning routes too. I see the same cars parked down the street when I leave for work, and then again when I get to my clients' houses. It's like they're tracking my every move. And I know why they're doing it, too. They're trying to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband. He's been having some health issues, and I do everything for him, I really do, but it's like they don't believe me. They think they need to spy on me to make sure I'm doing my job as a wife. It's exhausting, really, always looking over my shoulder, wondering who's watching me while I'm just trying to earn a living and take care of my home. But no one wants to believe me when I tell them. They just give me that look, you know the one, like I'm crazy or something. But I'm not. I know what's happening.\n\nDoctor: When you see these same cars following you, is there any part of you that wonders if maybe you could be mistaken, or are you completely certain that they are definitely watching you?\n\nPatient: Mistaken? No, no, Dr. [Name], I'm not mistaken. I know what you're getting at, it's what everyone gets at, that little hint of doubt they try to plant in your head. When you clean houses for a living, you develop an eye for detail. You notice if a vase has been moved half an inch, or if there's a scuff mark on the baseboard that wasn't there last Tuesday. So when I tell you I'm seeing the same cars, I mean I am seeing the exact same cars. There's this one, a dark blue sedan, always parked near the corner when I leave my house at 6:45 AM. And then, wouldn't you know it, when I pull up to Mrs. Higgins' house across town at 8:00, there it is again, just idling two doors down. And it's not just the cars, it's the people in them. I can feel them looking at me. \n\nLike I said before, I know exactly why they're doing it. It's the hospital people and the agency folks. They've teamed up or something because of my husband. He had that bad spell with his heart last year, and ever since then, I just know they've been whispering about me. They think because I work long hours, I'm neglecting him. But I'm not! I make his meals, I organize his pills, I do everything. But they don't believe it. So they watch the house, they watch my routes, they're just waiting for me to slip up so they can say, 'Aha! See? She's not taking proper care of him!' It's all a big test, a big surveillance operation just to catch me doing something wrong. So no, doctor, I'm not mistaken. I wish I was, I really do, because then maybe I could just go to work and come home without feeling like I'm on trial every single day. But I'm not imagining it. I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Does your husband also believe that you're being watched by the hospital people and the agency, or does he think these concerns are just in your head?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband... see, that's the thing that really breaks my heart, Dr. [Name]. Because you'd think, after thirty-two years of marriage, you'd think the person you share a bed with, the person you cook for and clean for and worry over every single day, you'd think he would be on your side. But he's just like the rest of them. He tells me I'm working too hard, that the chemicals I use at work are getting to my head, or that I'm just stressed about his health. Can you believe that? Stressed! Of course I'm stressed! But not because I'm imagining things. \n\nLike I told you, I know exactly what's happening. People keep acting like I'm making it all up, like I'm just imagining the whole thing, but I know the hospital people and those folks from the agency have been watching our house. They're watching my cleaning routes. They are actively doing this to see if I'm taking proper care of him. And I try to explain it to him, I sit him down at the kitchen table—the one I polished just that morning, mind you—and I say, \"Frank, look out the window, look at that blue car, they are waiting for me to leave for Mrs. Higgins' house.\" And he just sighs. He does this heavy sigh, rubs his face, and tells me to take a pill and lie down. He thinks it's all in my head. It makes me feel so alone, doctor. Because if your own husband doesn't believe you when you're trying to protect him, when you're trying to prove to the whole world that you are a good wife who takes proper care of him despite the agency and the hospital snooping around... well, who is going to believe you? It's just so frustrating, having to defend myself not just to the people spying on me, but to the very man I'm trying to care for.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the hospital people and the agency folks are watching you. Can you tell me - do you think they're working together as part of an organized plan, or are they acting separately? And what exactly do you think their ultimate goal is - what are they trying to accomplish by watching you?\n\nPatient: Oh, they are absolutely working together, Dr. [Name]. It’s a coordinated effort, I’m telling you. Think about it—how else would the hospital people know exactly what time I leave for my morning shift? The agency has to be giving them my schedule! It’s the only way it makes sense. I know I keep saying this, and I know people keep acting like I'm just imagining it, like I'm just making up stories out of thin air because I'm tired or stressed. But I know the hospital people and some folks from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes. I've seen them with my own two eyes, day in and day out. \n\nAnd as for what they want? Their ultimate goal? It's exactly what I was saying—they want to catch me slipping up. They want to prove that I'm not taking proper care of my husband. They think because I’m out scrubbing floors and cleaning toilets all day, doing back-breaking work for other people, that I’m somehow neglecting my own home and my own husband. But I’m not! I prep his meals before I even leave the house—low sodium, just like his cardiologist ordered. I sort his pills into that little plastic container with the days of the week on it, making sure the morning ones and the evening ones are perfectly separated. I do all the laundry, I keep the house spotless... I mean, I'm a professional cleaner, for heaven's sake, my house is immaculate! \n\nBut they don't care about any of that. They just want to find one little mistake. They want to catch me being five minutes late getting home from a job, or maybe they want to see if I forget to pick up his prescription, just so they can swoop in and say, \"Look, she’s an unfit wife, she can't handle it.\" Maybe the hospital wants to force him into one of those expensive care facilities, or maybe the agency just wants a reason to let me go because they think my home life is a liability now, I don't know the exact paperwork of it all. But I know they are working together to build some kind of case against me. It’s a full-time job just trying to outsmart them and keep my head on a swivel while I’m doing my actual job, you know? It’s just... it's not right. I work too hard for my family to be treated like some kind of criminal.\n\nDoctor: You've described this coordinated surveillance operation in quite a bit of detail. If I wanted to understand the full picture, could you walk me through exactly how you think the hospital and the agency are communicating with each other? How do you believe they're sharing your schedule and coordinating their surveillance?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's exactly what I try to lay out for people, Dr. [Name], because the way they communicate is all hidden in the paperwork and the schedules. I try to explain it, I really do, because it’s all connected. But it’s so frustrating, because when I try to explain what is actually happening, how they're passing the information back and forth, the words just... they run together because there are so many moving pieces. \n\nI start trying to explain about my husband's heart pills—the blue ones, you know, not the white ones, because the pharmacy changed the manufacturer on the 14th, and that refill date lines up exactly with when the hospital people must have called the agency. But then, to understand that, you have to understand the church schedule. Because Mrs. Higgins is on the altar guild, and the hospital auxiliary board meets on the exact same Tuesdays that the church prints the new bulletins, which is how the hospital knows when I'm going to be on the east side of town. And then... well, then it runs right into the numbers on my client list. The agency gives me these routing numbers for the houses I clean, and if you look at the last two digits of my client list, they match the hospital extension for the cardiology department! \n\nIt's all right there! But when I try to explain this to Frank, or to anyone really, how the heart pills connect to the church schedule and then to the numbers on my client list... people just sigh and say they can't follow me. They tell me I'm jumping around too much. But I'm not jumping around, I'm just trying to give them the whole picture! You have to see all the pieces to understand how the hospital knows where the agency is sending me. If I don't explain the church schedule, then the client numbers don't make sense, and if I leave out the heart pills, then the whole timeline falls apart. But nobody has the patience to just sit and listen to the details. They just wave their hands and say they can't follow what I'm saying, when really, they just aren't paying close enough attention to the connections I'm trying to show them.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at everything you've told me—the blue car, the hospital, the agency, the church schedule, the pill numbers—do these things feel like separate incidents that you're connecting together, or do they feel like one unified plan that was already organized from the start?\n\nPatient: Oh, it is absolutely one unified plan, doctor. From the very beginning, they had this all mapped out. It’s not just me picking up random puzzle pieces and trying to jam them together, no. They laid the whole puzzle out before I even knew we were playing a game. \n\nI mean, I know exactly why I'm sitting here talking to you. I'm here because people keep acting like I'm imagining it all, like I'm just a tired old woman who's losing her grip on reality from breathing in too much pine cleaner and floor wax all these years. But I know what I know. I know the hospital people and some of those folks from the agency have been watching our house and tracking my cleaning routes. And they didn't just decide to do that on a whim or as separate little things. It was organized from the start, all entirely designed to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband. \n\nThink about it, the blue car didn't just magically appear one day by accident. It showed up right when the pill numbers changed, which was right when the church schedule shifted. They set the whole board up to trap me from day one. And it just... it really drives me up the wall that my own husband, and everyone else for that matter, treats me like I'm making up fairy tales. If they would just sit down and look at the timeline, they would see that the hospital and the agency sat down together, looked at my life, and built this entire surveillance operation from the ground up just to prove I'm a bad wife. I'm not making connections that aren't there, I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the plan they put in motion! It's all one big, giant net they cast over my life, and I'm just trying my hardest not to get tangled up in it while I do my job and take care of Frank.\n\nDoctor: Besides seeing the cars and connecting these things together, do you ever hear voices or sounds that other people don't seem to hear, or do you experience anything else unusual like that?\n\nPatient: Well... you know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. I haven't really told Frank this part because he already looks at me like I've got two heads, but... yes, I do hear something. \n\nIt happens mostly when I'm in the kitchen. You know how a house gets when it's really, truly quiet? Like, early in the morning before the sun is even up and I'm getting Frank's low-sodium breakfast ready, or late at night after I've finished my last cleaning shift and I'm just wiping down our own counters because I can't settle my mind. But then... right by the kitchen window—the one over the sink, where I keep my African violets—I hear this voice. \n\nIt's a woman's voice. She'll say, plain as anything, that I need to check the locks, or she'll tell me to move Frank's pills. I'm here because people keep acting like I'm imagining it all, like I'm just a tired old woman who's losing her grip on reality from breathing in too much pine cleaner and floor wax all these years. And they didn't just decide to do that on a whim or as separate little things. It showed up right when the pill numbers changed, which was right when the church schedule shifted. They set the whole board up to trap me from day one. And it just... it really drives me up the wall that my own husband, and everyone else for that matter, treats me like I'm making up fairy tales. If they would just sit down and look at the timeline, they would see that the hospital and the agency sat down together, looked at my life, and built this entire surveillance operation from the ground up just to prove I'm a bad wife. I'm not making connections that aren't there, I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the plan they put in motion! And she's not whispering or mumbling, Dr. [Name], she is speaking clear as day, just as clear as you and I are talking right now. But there's nobody there. Just me and the quiet house. But I know what I heard! ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, it's not like... I'm not a puppet on a string or anything like that, doctor. It's not like she's taking control of my arms and forcing me to walk over to the counter. I have my own mind, and I know I could just stay right where I am and ignore her if I really, truly wanted to. 

Like, I could just keep washing the dishes. I use that blue Dawn dish soap, you know, because it's the only thing that really cuts through the grease when I make Frank's bacon. Even though I'm supposed to be keeping him on a low-sodium diet, I still make it for him on Sundays as a little treat, just to keep his spirits up. And my hands will be all sudsy, and she'll tell me to check the locks. And I'll stand there for a second, looking at the bubbles on my hands, and I'll realize that I could just keep scrubbing. I absolutely have that choice. I could just tell myself I'll do it later.

But then I start thinking about it, and I realize that she's actually making a lot of sense. Because why wouldn't I check the locks? With the hospital people and the agency coordinating everything, and that blue car showing up right when the church schedule changes, it would be downright foolish of me not to listen to her. It's just common sense, really. So I'll dry my hands off on my apron—I have this heavy cotton one with little yellow daisies on it that I wear when I'm doing the heavy kitchen work so I don't ruin my good blouses—and I'll walk over to the back door. 

Frank actually put that deadbolt in himself, back in ninety-eight or maybe ninety-nine, after the folks down the street had their garage broken into. It's a heavy brass one, and it sticks just a little bit if you don't pull the door handle up at the exact same time you turn the little knob. You have to really lift and jiggle it to make sure it's set right into the doorframe. So I go over and I jiggle it, and I make sure it's locked tight. Not because I didn't have a choice, but because when you know people are watching your house and tracking your cleaning routes, you'd have to be out of your mind not to make sure your own home is secure. So, yes, I choose to do it. It's just good advice, really, and I'd be a bad wife if I didn't take every single precaution to keep Frank safe from whatever they're planning with those schedules.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22.0, F22.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. [Name], I'm here because... well, honestly, it's because people keep acting like I'm making things up, like I'm just imagining the whole thing, but I know what I see. I've been a housekeeper for a long time, you know, scrubbing floors and dusting baseboards for the agency, and I know when things are out of place or when someone's been around. And lately, it's just been so obvious to me. The hospital people, and even some folks from my own cleaning agency, they've been... they've been watching our house. And not just the house, but my cleaning routes too. I see the same cars parked down the street when I leave for work, and then again when I get to my clients' houses. It's like they're tracking my every move. And I know why they're doing it, too. They're trying to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband. He's been having some health issues, and I do everything for him, I really do, but it's like they don't believe me. They think they need to spy on me to make sure I'm doing my job as a wife. It's exhausting, really, always looking over my shoulder, wondering who's watching me while I'm just trying to earn a living and take care of my home. But no one wants to believe me when I tell them. They just give me that look, you know the one, like I'm crazy or something. But I'm not. I know what's happening.\n\nDoctor: When you see these same cars following you, is there any part of you that wonders if maybe you could be mistaken, or are you completely certain that they are definitely watching you?\n\nPatient: Mistaken? No, no, Dr. [Name], I'm not mistaken. I know what you're getting at, it's what everyone gets at, that little hint of doubt they try to plant in your head. But you have to understand, I've been doing this for thirty years. Thirty years of driving the same routes, parking in the same driveways, noticing the little things. When you clean houses for a living, you develop an eye for detail. You notice if a vase has been moved half an inch, or if there's a scuff mark on the baseboard that wasn't there last Tuesday. So when I tell you I'm seeing the same cars, I mean I am seeing the exact same cars. There's this one, a dark blue sedan, always parked near the corner when I leave my house at 6:45 AM. And then, wouldn't you know it, when I pull up to Mrs. Higgins' house across town at 8:00, there it is again, just idling two doors down. And it's not just the cars, it's the people in them. I can feel them looking at me. \n\nLike I said before, I know exactly why they're doing it. It's the hospital people and the agency folks. They've teamed up or something because of my husband. He had that bad spell with his heart last year, and ever since then, I just know they've been whispering about me. They think because I work long hours, I'm neglecting him. But I'm not! I make his meals, I organize his pills, I do everything. But they don't believe it. So they watch the house, they watch my routes, they're just waiting for me to slip up so they can say, 'Aha! See? She's not taking proper care of him!' It's all a big test, a big surveillance operation just to catch me doing something wrong. So no, doctor, I'm not mistaken. I wish I was, I really do, because then maybe I could just go to work and come home without feeling like I'm on trial every single day. But I'm not imagining it. I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Does your husband also believe that you're being watched by the hospital people and the agency, or does he think these concerns are just in your head?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband... see, that's the thing that really breaks my heart, Dr. [Name]. Because you'd think, after thirty-two years of marriage, you'd think the person you share a bed with, the person you cook for and clean for and worry over every single day, you'd think he would be on your side. But he's just like the rest of them. He tells me I'm working too hard, that the chemicals I use at work are getting to my head, or that I'm just stressed about his health. Can you believe that? Stressed! Of course I'm stressed! But not because I'm imagining things. \n\nLike I told you, I know exactly what's happening. People keep acting like I'm making it all up, like I'm just imagining the whole thing, but I know the hospital people and those folks from the agency have been watching our house. They're watching my cleaning routes. They are actively doing this to see if I'm taking proper care of him. And I try to explain it to him, I sit him down at the kitchen table—the one I polished just that morning, mind you—and I say, \"Frank, look out the window, look at that blue car, they are waiting for me to leave for Mrs. Higgins' house.\" And he just sighs. He does this heavy sigh, rubs his face, and tells me to take a pill and lie down. He thinks it's all in my head. It makes me feel so alone, doctor. Because if your own husband doesn't believe you when you're trying to protect him, when you're trying to prove to the whole world that you are a good wife who takes proper care of him despite the agency and the hospital snooping around... well, who is going to believe you? It's just so frustrating, having to defend myself not just to the people spying on me, but to the very man I'm trying to care for.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the hospital people and the agency folks are watching you. Can you tell me - do you think they're working together as part of an organized plan, or are they acting separately? And what exactly do you think their ultimate goal is - what are they trying to accomplish by watching you?\n\nPatient: Oh, they are absolutely working together, Dr. [Name]. It’s a coordinated effort, I’m telling you. Think about it—how else would the hospital people know exactly what time I leave for my morning shift? The agency has to be giving them my schedule! It’s the only way it makes sense. I know I keep saying this, and I know people keep acting like I'm just imagining it, like I'm just making up stories out of thin air because I'm tired or stressed. But I know the hospital people and some folks from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes. I've seen them with my own two eyes, day in and day out. \n\nAnd as for what they want? Their ultimate goal? It's exactly what I was saying—they want to catch me slipping up. They want to prove that I'm not taking proper care of my husband. They think because I’m out scrubbing floors and cleaning toilets all day, doing back-breaking work for other people, that I’m somehow neglecting my own home and my own husband. But I’m not! I prep his meals before I even leave the house—low sodium, just like his cardiologist ordered. I sort his pills into that little plastic container with the days of the week on it, making sure the morning ones and the evening ones are perfectly separated. I do all the laundry, I keep the house spotless... I mean, I'm a professional cleaner, for heaven's sake, my house is immaculate! \n\nBut they don't care about any of that. They just want to find one little mistake. They want to catch me being five minutes late getting home from a job, or maybe they want to see if I forget to pick up his prescription, just so they can swoop in and say, \"Look, she’s an unfit wife, she can't handle it.\" Maybe the hospital wants to force him into one of those expensive care facilities, or maybe the agency just wants a reason to let me go because they think my home life is a liability now, I don't know the exact paperwork of it all. But I know they are working together to build some kind of case against me. It’s a full-time job just trying to outsmart them and keep my head on a swivel while I’m doing my actual job, you know? It’s just... it's not right. I work too hard for my family to be treated like some kind of criminal.\n\nDoctor: You've described this coordinated surveillance operation in quite a bit of detail. If I wanted to understand the full picture, could you walk me through exactly how you think the hospital and the agency are communicating with each other? How do you believe they're sharing your schedule and coordinating their surveillance?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's exactly what I try to lay out for people, Dr. [Name], because the way they communicate is all hidden in the paperwork and the schedules. I try to explain it, I really do, because it’s all connected. But it’s so frustrating, because when I try to explain what is actually happening, how they're passing the information back and forth, the words just... they run together because there are so many moving pieces. \n\nI start trying to explain about my husband's heart pills—the blue ones, you know, not the white ones, because the pharmacy changed the manufacturer on the 14th, and that refill date lines up exactly with when the hospital people must have called the agency. But then, to understand that, you have to understand the church schedule. Because Mrs. Higgins is on the altar guild, and the hospital auxiliary board meets on the exact same Tuesdays that the church prints the new bulletins, which is how the hospital knows when I'm going to be on the east side of town. And then... well, then it runs right into the numbers on my client list. The agency gives me these routing numbers for the houses I clean, and if you look at the last two digits of my client list, they match the hospital extension for the cardiology department! \n\nIt's all right there! But when I try to explain this to Frank, or to anyone really, how the heart pills connect to the church schedule and then to the numbers on my client list... people just sigh and say they can't follow me. They tell me I'm jumping around too much. But I'm not jumping around, I'm just trying to give them the whole picture! You have to see all the pieces to understand how the hospital knows where the agency is sending me. If I don't explain the church schedule, then the client numbers don't make sense, and if I leave out the heart pills, then the whole timeline falls apart. But nobody has the patience to just sit and listen to the details. They just wave their hands and say they can't follow what I'm saying, when really, they just aren't paying close enough attention to the connections I'm trying to show them.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at everything you've told me—the blue car, the hospital, the agency, the church schedule, the pill numbers—do these things feel like separate incidents that you're connecting together, or do they feel like one unified plan that was already organized from the start?\n\nPatient: Oh, it is absolutely one unified plan, doctor. From the very beginning, they had this all mapped out. It’s not just me picking up random puzzle pieces and trying to jam them together, no. They laid the whole puzzle out before I even knew we were playing a game. \n\nI mean, I know exactly why I'm sitting here talking to you. I'm here because people keep acting like I'm imagining it all, like I'm just a tired old woman who's losing her grip on reality from breathing in too much pine cleaner and floor wax all these years. But I know what I know. I know the hospital people and some of those folks from the agency have been watching our house and tracking my cleaning routes. And they didn't just decide to do that on a whim or as separate little things. It was organized from the start, all entirely designed to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband. \n\nThink about it, the blue car didn't just magically appear one day by accident. It showed up right when the pill numbers changed, which was right when the church schedule shifted. They set the whole board up to trap me from day one. And it just... it really drives me up the wall that my own husband, and everyone else for that matter, treats me like I'm making up fairy tales. If they would just sit down and look at the timeline, they would see that the hospital and the agency sat down together, looked at my life, and built this entire surveillance operation from the ground up just to prove I'm a bad wife. I'm not making connections that aren't there, I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the plan they put in motion! It's all one big, giant net they cast over my life, and I'm just trying my hardest not to get tangled up in it while I do my job and take care of Frank.\n\nDoctor: Besides seeing the cars and connecting these things together, do you ever hear voices or sounds that other people don't seem to hear, or do you experience anything else unusual like that?\n\nPatient: Well... you know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. I haven't really told Frank this part because he already looks at me like I've got two heads, but... yes, I do hear something. \n\nIt happens mostly when I'm in the kitchen. You know how a house gets when it's really, truly quiet? Like, early in the morning before the sun is even up and I'm getting Frank's low-sodium breakfast ready, or late at night after I've finished my last cleaning shift and I'm just wiping down our own counters because I can't settle my mind. The house will be completely, entirely silent. You could hear a pin drop. But then... right by the kitchen window—the one over the sink, where I keep my African violets—I hear this voice. \n\nIt's a woman's voice. And she's not whispering or mumbling, Dr. [Name], she is speaking clear as day, just as clear as you and I are talking right now. It sounds exactly like she is standing right there on the linoleum next to me. And she tells me things I need to do. She'll say, plain as anything, that I need to check the locks, or she'll tell me to move Frank's pills. She'll say, \"Check the back door,\" or \"Move his pills off that counter.\" \n\nAnd I'll stop dead in my tracks, usually with a dish towel still in my hand, and I'll look around, expecting to see someone right there in my kitchen. But there's nobody there. Just me and the quiet house. But I know what I heard! It's so real that I actually go and check the deadbolt on the back door, and I'll take his little plastic pill container and move it to the other side of the kitchen, just like she said. Because, well, with everything else going on, with the hospital and the agency watching me, you just can't be too careful, right? But it really startles me, because I'll just be standing there in the dead quiet, and suddenly this woman is right there at the window telling me to secure the house and hide the medication. I just... I don't know how she gets so close without me seeing her, but I hear her, clear as a bell.\n\nDoctor: When this woman's voice tells you to check the locks or move Frank's pills, do you feel like you absolutely have to do what she says, like you don't have a choice? Or could you choose not to do it if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's not like... I'm not a puppet on a string or anything like that, doctor. It's not like she's taking control of my arms and forcing me to walk over to the counter. I have my own mind, and I know I could just stay right where I am and ignore her if I really, truly wanted to. \n\nLike, I could just keep washing the dishes. I use that blue Dawn dish soap, you know, because it's the only thing that really cuts through the grease when I make Frank's bacon. Even though I'm supposed to be keeping him on a low-sodium diet, I still make it for him on Sundays as a little treat, just to keep his spirits up. And my hands will be all sudsy, and she'll tell me to check the locks. And I'll stand there for a second, looking at the bubbles on my hands, and I'll realize that I could just keep scrubbing. I absolutely have that choice. I could just tell myself I'll do it later.\n\nBut then I start thinking about it, and I realize that she's actually making a lot of sense. Because why wouldn't I check the locks? With the hospital people and the agency coordinating everything, and that blue car showing up right when the church schedule changes, it would be downright foolish of me not to listen to her. It's just common sense, really. So I'll dry my hands off on my apron—I have this heavy cotton one with little yellow daisies on it that I wear when I'm doing the heavy kitchen work so I don't ruin my good blouses—and I'll walk over to the back door. \n\nFrank actually put that deadbolt in himself, back in ninety-eight or maybe ninety-nine, after the folks down the street had their garage broken into. It's a heavy brass one, and it sticks just a little bit if you don't pull the door handle up at the exact same time you turn the little knob. You have to really lift and jiggle it to make sure it's set right into the doorframe. So I go over and I jiggle it, and I make sure it's locked tight. Not because I didn't have a choice, but because when you know people are watching your house and tracking your cleaning routes, you'd have to be out of your mind not to make sure your own home is secure. So, yes, I choose to do it. It's just good advice, really, and I'd be a bad wife if I didn't take every single precaution to keep Frank safe from whatever they're planning with those schedules.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
```json
{
  "candidates": ["F22.0"],
  "note": "Patient now reports command hallucinations (auditory hallucinations with a female voice giving specific instructions) in addition to persecutory delusions. The presence of both delusions and hallucinations, combined with intact reality testing ability (she could choose not to follow the voice but rationalizes why she should), indicates F22.0 (Delusional disorder, somatic type progressing to mixed type with psychotic features) rather than F22.9. The systematized nature of the delusions with incorporation of multiple unrelated elements (pills, church schedule, client numbers) and the auditory hallucinations meeting criteria for a psychotic disorder. F22.0 remains most appropriate as the primary presentation involves delusions of reference and persecution regarding her husband's care, with secondary command hallucinations.",
  "is_final": false
}
```

But then... right by the kitchen window—the one over the sink, where I keep my African violets—I hear this voice. \n\nIt's a woman's voice. And she's not whispering or mumbling, Dr. [Name], she is speaking clear as day, just as clear as you and I are talking right now. It sounds exactly like she is standing right there on the linoleum next to me. And she tells me things I need to do. She'll say, plain as anything, that I need to check the locks, or she'll tell me to move Frank's pills. She'll say, \"Check the back door,\" or \"Move his pills off that counter.\" \n\nAnd I'll stop dead in my tracks, usually with a dish towel still in my hand, and I'll look around, expecting to see someone right there in my kitchen. But there's nobody there. Just me and the quiet house. But I know what I heard! It's so real that I actually go and check the deadbolt on the back door, and I'll take his little plastic pill container and move it to the other side of the kitchen, just like she said. Because, well, with everything else going on, with the hospital and the agency watching me, you just can't be too careful, right? But it really startles me, because I'll just be standing there in the dead quiet, and suddenly this woman is right there at the window telling me to secure the house and hide the medication. I just... I don't know how she gets so close without me seeing her, but I hear her, clear as a bell.\n\nDoctor: When this woman's voice tells you to check the locks or move Frank's pills, do you feel like you absolutely have to do what she says, like you don't have a choice? Or could you choose not to do it if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's not like... I'm not a puppet on a string or anything like that, doctor. I use that blue Dawn dish soap, you know, because it's the only thing that really cuts through the grease when I make Frank's bacon. Even though I'm supposed to be keeping him on a low-sodium diet, I still make it for him on Sundays as a little treat, just to keep his spirits up. And my hands will be all sudsy, and she'll tell me to check the locks. And I'll stand there for a second, looking at the bubbles on my hands, and I'll realize that I could just keep scrubbing. I absolutely have that choice. I could just tell myself I'll do it later.\n\nBut then I start thinking about it, and I realize that she's actually making a lot of sense. Because why wouldn't I check the locks? It's just common sense, really. So I'll dry my hands off on my apron—I have this heavy cotton one with little yellow daisies on it that I wear when I'm doing the heavy kitchen work so I don't ruin my good blouses—and I'll walk over to the back door. \n\nFrank actually put that deadbolt in himself, back in ninety-eight or maybe ninety-nine, after the folks down the street had their garage broken into. It's a heavy brass one, and it sticks just a little bit if you don't pull the door handle up at the exact same time you turn the little knob. You have to really lift and jiggle it to make sure it's set right into the doorframe. So I go over and I jiggle it, and I make sure it's locked tight. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... no. I mean, why would I think it's coming from inside my own mind? That just doesn't make any sense to me, doctor. I know what my own thoughts sound like. My thoughts sound like me. When I'm thinking about, say, what aisle the Pine-Sol is in at the grocery store, or whether I need to pick up more of those heavy-duty trash bags for work, that's my voice in my head. But this... this is entirely different. 

Like I was saying, it happens when the house is just dead quiet. I mean, the kind of quiet where you can hear the refrigerator hum kick on from two rooms away. And I'll be standing there, maybe wiping down the counter—I always use a damp microfiber cloth because paper towels just push the dust around, you know? And right there, near the kitchen window, the one right over the sink where my African violets are sitting... I hear her. 

And it is not a thought. It's a woman's voice, and she is speaking out loud, clear as day. "Check the locks," she'll say. Or "Move his pills." And it sounds exactly like she is standing right there on the linoleum next to me. If it were just in my head, it wouldn't sound like it was coming from the window, would it? It wouldn't make me jump out of my skin and look over my shoulder expecting to see a person standing there in my kitchen. It's an actual voice, Doctor [Name]. I hear it with my ears, not my brain. It's just as real as you asking me that question right now. So no, I don't think it's just my mind playing tricks on me. I know what I hear, and I hear her, clear as if she's standing right there beside me.
